Feature Explanation

This feature fires on very short BPE fragments (single letters, 1–2-char pieces, digits or symbols) rather than full words—i.e. initials, acronyms, month/day abbreviations, equation or code tokens and other tiny subword or metadata markers.

Feature Explanation

Feature 1 (“…felt ‘intimidated’ by Jeff Bridges” etc.): This feature flags passages expressing a subject’s sense of being overwhelmed or daunted by a more experienced or eminent person (i.e. “intimidation” narratives). Feature 2 (“Empowering Women to Embrace Their God-Given Design…,” “First moments One: A Poem,” “My body grows…”): This feature marks chapter- or section-level titles in inspirational/spiritual or pregnancy‐focused prose and poetry. Feature 3 (“News,” “UPDATE: Tips…,” “Buck Danny,” “Cold Weekend,” product guides, blog posts, transcript headings, etc.): This feature detects document‐ or section‐level headings and headlines across diverse genres (news articles, blog entries, transcripts, product guides, etc.).

Feature Explanation

This feature is a general “sentence‐boundary” detector: it lights up on end-of-utterance markers (periods, question marks, exclamation points, closing brackets or quotes—even language-specific full‐stops), and more weakly on line-break or hyphenation points. In other words, it signals that the model is hitting the end of a sentence or clause.

Feature Explanation

This neuron fires on “index‐style” tokens used to label or enumerate items—e.g. standalone numbers (years, chapter or image indices, array or list positions, exponent digits, numeric constants) and similarly short boundary markers (like the “.c” file‐extension token). In other words, it picks out tokens whose primary role is to index or identify entries in text or code.

Feature Explanation

This neuron detects Title-Case headings and labels—i.e. the capitalized names, titles or section/menu headings (often followed by punctuation) that mark the start of an article, menu entry, code/license header, product name, etc.

#1565c0;">Feature Explanation</h3> <p>This neuron seems to be a “chunk-boundary” detector: it fires on the last meaningful token of a content chunk just before a small, highly predictable unit—whether that be a function word (“as _long_ as,” “as _far_ as,” “Posted _by_”), punctuation (“7_:_00 pm”), or the next segment of a compound identifier or name (the “DA” in WebDAV, “ST” in CROSSTOOL, “Late” in LaTeX, “ant” in Qantas, “ven” in Provenzano, “ic” in “.icn,” “A” in a GUID, etc.).
